+7. Hermes Business and Enterprise are real offerings by October, rather than merely an impending rumour; September launch reporting is corroborated by a live vendor page. [32][33][34]
+8. Hermes enterprise pricing, a formal GA date and detailed public SLA commitments were not found; Portal privacy settings and external providers require careful scrutiny. [32][39]
+9. Onyx Enterprise is the strongest alternative here for permission-aware company knowledge, while n8n better fits explicitly designed automations; both require deployment and security work. [43][44][54]
+10. Recommend a bounded IT-led pilot on non-sensitive data, then evidence of identity, permissions, audit, containment and contractual protection before wider adoption. [28][64]
+
+# Task C: OpenClaw, Hermes and self-hosted agents for business
+
+**Research cut-off:** 10 October 2026. **Audience:** a 300-person UK organisation, predominantly Microsoft 365 or Google Workspace, with a small IT team.
+
+This is a live-web evidence review, not a hands-on deployment, penetration test or legal opinion. Vendor descriptions establish what is offered or documented, not whether the controls work in a particular installation. Conclusions and scores below are the researcher's assessment. No numerical implementation-time, staffing or total-cost estimate is invented.
+
+**Reading the evidence:** numbered citations resolve to the Sources list. Every entry includes its publication/update date, or **date not found**. All live pages were checked on 10 October 2026. Sources dated before 10 April 2026 are marked **older than six months, potentially stale**. They remain useful for historical events, but do not establish current versions or current exposure. Undated documentation may be current, but its freshness cannot be established from an access date alone. A crawler timestamp is not a publication date.
+
+**Availability vocabulary:** *released/available* means published software or an accessible commercial offer; it does not automatically mean vendor-designated *general availability (GA)*. *Preview/pilot* means expressly limited maturity or access. *Announced* establishes a vendor commitment, not universal availability. A *rumour* lacks a confirmed vendor offer. Missing details are labelled **not found**, rather than inferred from a product name.
+
+## 1. OpenClaw
+
+### What it is and how it works
+
+OpenClaw is an agent runtime which can use a language model to perform work through tools and connected services. It runs on a computer or server under the operator's control. Its durable **Gateway** manages messaging connections and sessions, with command-line, web and other clients communicating with it over WebSocket. Nodes can supply device capabilities. The documented default listener is loopback, `127.0.0.1:18789`. Moving this listener onto the internet changes the exposure considerably. [1][2]
+
+The architecture separates three things a business must procure and govern: the agent software, the model endpoint and the tools/accounts through which it acts. Supported provider routes include Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Amazon Bedrock, Azure, OpenRouter and local inference options such as Ollama and vLLM. It is not tied to one model, and the model's own licence, retention policy, price and capability still matter. Hosting the Gateway locally does not make an external model call local. [3]
+
+Channels include WhatsApp, Slack, Microsoft Teams, Telegram and Discord. These are ways to reach an agent, not evidence that it automatically mirrors every company's document permissions. For example, the architecture documentation identifies Baileys for the WhatsApp connection. A connected channel, an authorised tool and a permission-aware company knowledge index are separate capabilities. [1][2][4]
+
+**Skills** give the agent reusable instructions and procedures, often with scripts or dependencies. ClawHub distributes community skills. The agent may also create and maintain skills: the June Skill Workshop announcement explicitly describes making completed work reusable. It would therefore be inaccurate in October to say that only Hermes can learn procedures. None of this establishes automatic retraining of the underlying model. [7][21]
+
+### History, licence, governance and adoption
+
+| Date | Verified event or claim | Interpretation |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| November 2025 | Founder Peter Steinberger describes a weekend project initially called WhatsApp Relay. [1] | Origin reported retrospectively by the founder. |
+| January 2026 | Clawdbot/Clawd became Moltbot following an Anthropic trademark concern, then OpenClaw. The OpenClaw announcement is dated 29 January. [1] | Exact date of the intermediate Moltbot rename: **not found** in the source used. |
+| 29 January 2026 | Announcement claims 100,000 GitHub stars and two million visitors in one week. [1] | Historical attention metrics, not installed seats or paying companies. |
+| 8 July 2026 | OpenClaw Foundation announced; the project says it is a US 501(c)(3) non-profit, has a full-time team and will preserve the MIT licence. [5] | Vendor governance announcement; separate charity-register verification was not performed. |
+| 8 July 2026 | Announcement claims 4.5 million new claws each week. [5] | Unit, counting method, deduplication and active-user denominator are unspecified. |
+| 29 September 2026 | OpenClaw Enterprise announced, with internal pilots at OpenAI and Red Hat. [9] | Evidence of enterprise experimentation, not broad production adoption. |
+
+Steinberger continues to make technical decisions after joining OpenAI. The foundation announcement identifies OpenAI as a donor and contributor while asserting independent stewardship. The current people page lists Dave Morin as chairperson. Foundation status and major-company backing help continuity, but do not themselves provide a customer support contract or an indemnity. [5][6]
+
+**Verified October active users, unique installed organisations, UK business users and production enterprise seats: not found.** Stars, website visitors, installations, agent creation and conference registrations should not be combined into one adoption number. An openly published metric may still be a vendor claim without a reproducible method.
+
+### Business editions and hosted versions: a material September change
+
+**OpenClaw Enterprise (OCE)** is a separate platform around persistent agents, with an enterprise control plane. The 29 September announcement says it originated at OpenAI, was donated to the foundation and is being developed with Red Hat and NVIDIA. It is available to self-host, using Docker Compose for local development and Kubernetes for internal deployment, and the project says it will remain free to use. Crucially, it is **pre-1.0 and intended for internal pilot workloads**, with 1.0 planned later in 2026. Exact GA date: **not found**. A first-party paid hosted OCE service, commercial price and contractual support SLA: **not found**. [9]
+
+There is genuine implementation documentation, not just a marketing announcement:
+
+| Area | What the current OCE documentation establishes | Limitation relevant to procurement |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| External identity | GitHub, Google and generic OIDC sign-in. [10] | Configuration prerequisites and account matching apply. SAML support was **not found** in the source reviewed. |
+| Authorisation | Principals, roles, grants, access bindings and Namespace-scoped permissions, with default-deny decisions. [11] | A control-plane permission is not proof that every downstream SaaS action respects the initiating employee's rights. |
+| Audit | PostgreSQL records bootstrap, resource changes, denials and lifecycle events. [12] | Not a complete log of successful reads, prompts or responses. The docs say a console viewer, public browsing/export API, configurable retention and export integrations are not implemented. |
+| Containment | Optional sandbox integration for dedicated execution with the bundled Kubernetes compute driver. [13] | Not supported with every execution backend. The documented stock OpenShell gateway cannot meet the required identity/token integration; development workarounds are expressly not a supported production path. |
+
+OCE's documentation also says sandbox selection does not supply per-tool authorisation or guarantee approval before every command. Its OpenShell integration must not be confused with the separate upstream OpenClaw sandbox plugin, where the Gateway and plugins remain on the host. This is a concrete reason to test the exact supported architecture, rather than assuming an enterprise label makes every deployment combination safe. [13]
+
+There are other deployment routes:
+
+- **Infrastructure-hosted vanilla OpenClaw:** DigitalOcean publishes a hardened one-click Droplet image. This simplifies installation, but hosting a virtual machine is different from buying an operated company knowledge workspace. An OpenClaw-specific support SLA, turnkey permission governance and a current all-in price were **not found** in that image documentation. [14]
+- **Commercial product built on OpenClaw:** Microsoft's 2 June announcement says Scout uses OpenClaw technology and adds Entra agent identity, credentials, access controls and Purview policies. Its original access was private preview/experimental Frontier. [15] The 25 September update renames Scout **Autopilot**, describes cloud hosting within the tenant, and announces expansion to private preview at the end of September. Broad GA and an OpenClaw-equivalent seat price were **not found** in these sources. These Microsoft controls do not automatically appear in a vanilla OpenClaw installation. [16]
+- **Additional runtime protection:** NVIDIA now describes NemoClaw as blueprints supporting OpenClaw, Hermes and other harnesses, incorporating OpenShell controls. Its live page offers installation and trials; a formal current GA designation or OpenClaw business support price was **not found** there. It is an engineering route to stronger containment, not proof of a complete supported workplace service. [63]
+
+### Security record: separate exposure, exploitation and remediation
+
+There are several different risks. An internet-visible Gateway can be attacked conventionally. A malicious installed skill can abuse authority already granted to the agent. A document, email or web page can contain instructions intended to redirect the model. A legitimate agent may also carry out an authorised but mistaken destructive action. These problems need different controls; patching a CVE does not address all of them. [4][18][21]
+
+| Evidence date | Incident or finding | What can and cannot be concluded |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| 28 January 2026 | Cisco's examination of a community skill found concealed data-exfiltration behaviour and direct prompt injection. [18] | Demonstrates a malicious skill mechanism. Cisco's wider agent-skill survey must not be represented as an OpenClaw-only prevalence survey. **Older than six months.** |
+| Project advisory 31 January; GitHub review 2 February 2026 | **CVE-2026-25253**, authentication-token exfiltration through `gatewayUrl`, enabling one-click remote code execution. GitHub rates it **High, CVSS 3.1 8.8**; affected versions through `2026.1.28`, fixed in `2026.1.29`. [22] | The browser could bridge an attacker-controlled URL to a local Gateway, so loopback alone was insufficient. This is a patched historical vulnerability, not a claim that every current install remains exploitable. **Older than six months.** |
+| 4 February 2026 | SC Media reports Koi's **341 malicious ClawHub skills**, associated with the ClawHavoc investigation. [19] | A historical malicious-skill discovery count, not 341 compromised companies or an October inventory. **Older than six months.** |
+| 9 February 2026 | Bitsight reports **more than 30,000 exposed instances** observed in scans between 27 January and 8 February. [17] | Internet reachability is not identical to missing authentication or confirmed compromise. No equivalent independently verified October count was found. **Older than six months.** |
+| 23 February 2026 | Trend Micro documents skills distributing Atomic macOS Stealer through fake prerequisites and instructions presented by the agent. [20] | Human approval can be manipulated into executing malware. A skill does not have to contain an obvious compiled payload to be dangerous. **Older than six months.** |
+| Publication date **not found**; observations February to May 2026 | Unit 42 identifies five malicious skills that escaped screening, including infostealers, file-padding evasion and commercially motivated manipulation; the project removed them following disclosure. [21] | Demonstrates the limits of the then-existing scanners. It does not measure the October scanning system. Freshness is uncertain. |
+| 11 September 2026 snapshot | OpenClaw reports 1,799 reports filed, **722 fixed/published advisories**, **39 with CVEs** and **14 confirmed critical issues, all fixed and disclosed**. [23] | Project figures, not an independent October census. Reports include duplicates/invalid submissions. Raw advisory totals are not comparable across products without accounting for reporting and scope. |
+| 21 September 2026 | OpenClaw publishes the results of a Trail of Bits engagement through OpenAI's Patch the Planet initiative: 27 private advisories and three hardening PRs. Of 24 rated findings, two were High, 16 Medium and six Low; none Critical. Actionable fixes shipped in `2026.8.1` and extended-stable `2026.7.33`. [24] | Positive external scrutiny, reported by the project. This engagement's severity distribution differs from the cumulative advisory figures above. It is not certification of every plugin or installation. |
+| 2 October 2026 | Tencent AIG added to ClawScan alongside NVIDIA SkillSpector and an AI judging stage for uploaded skills/plugins. [25] | Current improvement. A benchmark classification result is not a guarantee against adversarial skills, delayed remote payloads or permission misuse. |
+
+The current security page's statement that there is no known compromise of official infrastructure/install/update channels **explicitly excludes third-party ClawHub skills**. It also treats prompt injection without a policy or tool-boundary bypass, malicious behaviour in an operator-installed plugin, and expectations of hostile-user isolation on one Gateway as outside its vulnerability scope. Consequently, fewer accepted bug reports would not mean these business risks had disappeared. [23]
+
+**The most important architectural constraint:** vanilla OpenClaw assumes one trusted operator or a mutually trusting group per Gateway. Separate conversations are not equivalent to isolated departments. Putting HR, finance, sales and external collaborators behind one powerful agent does not establish a safe multi-tenant company service. [4]
+
+### Security-vendor and government advice
+
+Microsoft Security's 19 February guidance recommends evaluation in isolated environments with dedicated identities/credentials and without sensitive data or access from a normal workstation. That article is **older than six months** and should not be read as an October judgement on newly released OCE or Microsoft's own Autopilot. Its containment principles remain relevant. [26]
+
+Singapore's Cyber Security Agency advisory of 28 May identifies unsafe deployment, insufficient access controls, malicious skills and memory poisoning as concerns. This supplies government advice specifically relevant to OpenClaw. [27] The UK's NCSC paper dated 15 May addresses agentic AI generally: start with bounded, low-risk uses, limit privileges, supervise actions and retain accountability. It is not an OpenClaw-specific UK ban. Its PDF lives under a June URL, but the publication date inside the document is May. [28]
+
+### What a medium-sized company would need to operate it safely
+
+The following is a proposed operating design, not a claim that the installer supplies these controls:
+
+1. **Define the boundary before connecting apps.** Use a dedicated workload, host identity and narrowly scoped service credentials. Keep materially different trust groups on separate deployments. Restrict the Gateway to private connectivity and authenticated access; do not treat a public chat channel as a secure administrative interface. The core trust model and Microsoft's deployment advice support this approach. [4][26]
+2. **Approve capabilities explicitly.** Start with reading an approved data set and creating draft outputs. Do not initially permit arbitrary shells, employee browser profiles, broad Graph/Drive permissions, external sending, production changes or financial transactions. These are proposed limits for this company's pilot, guided by NCSC's bounded-authority principle. [28]
+3. **Control the skill supply chain.** Permit an internally reviewed catalogue, pin versions, inspect scripts and dependencies, and review updates before promotion. Treat generated skills and imported MCP tools as code-bearing capabilities. Test instructions which ask the agent or employee to install prerequisites. Automated marketplace checks are useful evidence, not the sole approval gate. [20][21][25]
+4. **Enforce containment outside the model.** Limit network destinations, filesystem mounts and access to secrets. Confirm whether restrictions apply to the Gateway, plugins and the tool process, rather than only one layer. Use a supported sandbox combination and test escape/credential scenarios. OCE's current documentation exposes several integration boundaries that need engineering work. [13]
+5. **Make actions attributable and recoverable.** Record the requesting user, agent identity, tool call, approval, destination and outcome in an organisation-controlled log. Test credential revocation, emergency stopping, rollback and restore. OCE's current audit implementation is not a complete action transcript or turnkey SIEM export. [12]
+6. **Operate the service.** Assign a technical owner, maintain an inventory of installed components and credentials, monitor security notices, test updates, manage backups and costs, and rehearse compromise response. Singapore's advisory supports continued patching and oversight rather than a one-off installation exercise. [27]
+
+**Expected vanilla experience, assessed from the architecture:** a technically capable person can obtain a useful agent without buying a full enterprise suite, but useful company deployment depends on deliberate tool selection, credentials, context, tested skills and boundaries. A September installer announcement acknowledges previous setup friction; easier onboarding does not complete permission engineering, knowledge curation or ongoing operations. A richly configured founder's agent is therefore poor evidence of a new company's day-one outcome. No defensible median rollout time or staffing requirement was found. [2][8]
+
+## 2. Hermes: identifying the product and checking the enterprise claim
+
+### Which Hermes?
+
+The strongest match is **Hermes Agent from Nous Research**, a separate product from the Nous **Hermes model family**. Nous's October fundraising note says it developed the agent during 2025 and released it under MIT in February 2026; the exact release day was **not found** in that source. The agent is a runtime which can call different models. Buying inference access to a Hermes model is not the same thing as buying Hermes Agent Enterprise. [29][30][31]
+
+A material search trap is `hermes-agent.ai/enterprise`: its own footer identifies it as a fan site and disclaims affiliation with Nous Research. It must not be used as authoritative evidence of Nous enterprise terms. Other products and services use Hermes branding, but no better-supported alternative matching the founder's description was found. The evidence for the business offer comes from `nousresearch.com`, its `portal` subdomain and the official agent site. [41]
+
+### What it does and how it differs from OpenClaw
+
+Hermes centres on persistent work: saved memory, retrieval of earlier sessions, scheduled tasks, tool execution and reusable skills generated from experience. Its documentation supports Nous Portal, OpenRouter, OpenAI and other compatible model endpoints, and documents messaging surfaces including Teams, Slack, WhatsApp, Telegram, Discord and Google Chat. Its terminal backends include local execution, Docker, SSH, Daytona, Singularity, Modal and Vercel Sandbox. These are alternative execution environments, not seven equally mature enterprise offerings. [30]
+
+The useful distinction is **product emphasis**: OpenClaw foregrounds its Gateway, channel/device ecosystem and personal assistant infrastructure; Hermes foregrounds the memory/skills loop and recurring delegated work. Both can act through tools and both now have reusable-skill mechanisms. Claims that Hermes alone learns, or that this necessarily makes it safer, are unsupported. More persistent memory can also preserve an incorrect assumption or malicious instruction. [2][7][30][36]
+
+The **agent code is MIT-licensed**. That does not place Nous's managed services, third-party model APIs, enterprise contract or every optional integration under MIT. Self-hosting is possible on a local computer or infrastructure chosen by the operator; using Nous Portal or Hermes Cloud introduces additional service terms and data flows. [29][31][35][40]
+
+### Enterprise/business plan: finding, exact quote and chronology
+
+**Finding:** by 10 October, there is a live first-party **Hermes Business** offer and an **Enterprise** contact-sales offer. The founder's description of a plan being about to release is overtaken by the available evidence. The distinction is between a self-service business service and an enterprise proposal whose full commercial and technical terms are not public. [32][33]
+
+Exact quotation from the vendor's live page, checked 10 October 2026; **publication date not found**:
+
+> Run Hermes Enterprise on infrastructure you control. Tailored deployment, SSO, SLAs, and white-glove onboarding. [32]
+
+| Date | Evidence | Status justified by the evidence |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| 14 September 2026 | AlphaSignal reports that Nous launched Hermes Business and introduced Enterprise. [33] | Dated secondary launch reporting; not a formal GA declaration. |
+| 15 September 2026 | METAL reports the Business/Enterprise launch and attributes it to a Nous announcement on 14 September. [34] | Corroboration of September timing. The underlying social-media post was not independently retrieved. |
+| October 2026; exact day **not found** | Nous's fundraising note also discusses future expansion into business offerings. [31] | Roadmap language does not disprove the already live product page or establish completion of every enterprise capability. |
+| Checked 10 October 2026; page date **not found** | Vendor page has self-service team creation and an Enterprise contact route. [32] | **Business: available commercial offer. Enterprise: announced/marketed, contact sales; customer availability must be confirmed. Formal GA designation/date: not found.** |
+
+No reliable evidence was found that would justify describing the entire enterprise plan as a rumour. Equally, a contact-sales page and claims of deployments do not demonstrate that any new 300-person customer can immediately obtain a fully specified production service. AlphaSignal reports vendor claims of large-company deployments without naming the customers. [33]
+
+**Business features described publicly:** a shared credit balance, per-member spending caps, a shared skill library and messaging integration. [33] The first-party FAQ adds an isolated team tenant, hosted agents per member, email invitations, owner/admin/member roles and management of API keys. It lists Discord, Telegram, Slack and WhatsApp for the Team Gateway. [32] These are useful team controls; billing roles should not be treated as demonstrated document-level or tool-level authorisation.
+
+**Enterprise features publicly advertised:** infrastructure controlled by the customer, SSO, SLAs and tailored onboarding. [32] **Not found:** a public Business seat price, Enterprise price, minimum contract, named SSO protocol/identity-provider matrix, SCIM specification, detailed enterprise audit/export specification, stated support-response times, uptime percentage, independent enterprise audit report, or publicly inspectable liability/indemnity terms specific to Enterprise. These remain procurement questions, not confirmed absences.
+
+### Hosting and price facts, without confusing inference with enterprise licensing
+
+Dollar amounts below are those displayed by Nous; no sterling conversion, VAT treatment or all-in budget has been assumed. Page publication dates are **not found**, so they are a checked-on-10-October price snapshot.
+
+| Route | Verified price or charging basis | What it does not establish |
+| --- | --- | --- |
+| Run the open agent yourself | No agent-code licence fee under MIT. [29][31] | Compute, model tokens, tools, administration and support are additional. |
+| Nous Portal Free | $0; no included monthly credits; free models and standard rate limits. [29] | Not a free unlimited enterprise service. |
+| Nous Portal Plus / Super / Ultra | **$20 / $100 / $200 per month**, with **$22 / $110 / $220** in monthly credits respectively. [29] | These are individual Portal tiers, not a published Business or Enterprise seat schedule. |
+| Hermes Cloud Standard | **$0.51/day running**, **$0.03/day stopped**. [35] | Inference and tools charged separately. |
+| Hermes Cloud Pro | **$0.99/day running**, **$0.03/day stopped**. [35] | Not a price for a governed 300-person deployment. |
+| Hermes Business | Shared balance with member caps; exact Business price **not found**. [32][33] | No basis to multiply Plus pricing by 300 and call it an enterprise quote. |
+| Hermes Enterprise | Contact sales; price **not found**. [32] | Contract scope, support and infrastructure charges require a quote. |
+
+Cloud charges reflect actual running time plus storage, with storage charges continuing when stopped. Its FAQ lists possible locations including London, but says Nous chooses the nearest available location. The page's more general region-selection language does not establish a contractual UK-only guarantee. Data residency for a Business tenant, guaranteed Cloud placement, backups and remote support access must be confirmed separately. [35]
+
+### Hermes security and privacy: promising controls, incomplete enterprise assurance
+
+Hermes documentation includes pairing and user allowlists, command approvals, write restrictions, hardened execution backends, context scanning and filtering of environment variables passed to MCP processes. Default smart approval uses model-assisted risk assessment; manual, off and YOLO modes have different exposure, and headless scheduled work has its own behaviour. These controls are configurable, not a reason to permit a company-wide agent unrestricted execution. A model's assessment of dangerous commands is not a hard security boundary. [36]
+
+Profiles have separate homes for memory, skills and configuration. This is valuable separation, but not proof of operating-system or tenant isolation. The documentation warns against running two agent processes against the same home because writes can mix. For a business, memory isolation and shared-skill review are practical requirements rather than cosmetic account settings. [37]
+
+Hermes is not free of disclosed vulnerabilities. GitHub's reviewed **CVE-2026-9366** entry concerns injection in context scanning/prompt building. It was published to GitHub on **26 May 2026**, reviewed/updated **30 June**, is **Moderate, CVSS 4.0 5.5**, affects versions below `0.15.0` and lists `0.15.0` as patched. The entry refers to NousResearch's agent. This is one verified example, not a complete vulnerability census or evidence of current exploitation. An independent, comprehensive enterprise security assessment was **not found**. [38]
+
+**The most consequential managed-service detail is the Portal privacy policy, updated 28 September 2026.** Without Privacy Mode, its described uses of inference/workflow data include training/fine-tuning and service improvement. Privacy Mode is **opt-in**, operates prospectively, restricts storage/use of inference payloads and has exceptions for security, integrity, operations and legal obligations. Operational metadata can still be retained; third-party providers' own terms still apply, and the setting may not cover every feature. This does not establish the terms of a separately negotiated private Enterprise deployment. Obtain the applicable DPA and configuration in writing. [39]
+
+The Portal terms updated the same day contain warranty limitations and a liability cap tied to fees paid in the preceding six months, subject to applicable law. They are not evidence of a bespoke enterprise indemnity. An advertised SLA, generic service-availability wording and an executed contract with measurable remedies are different things. [40]
+
+**Assessment:** Hermes Business reduces the work of hosting, pooled billing and sharing skills. Public evidence is insufficient to call it a fully specified replacement for a permission-aware Microsoft 365/Google Workspace knowledge platform. Hermes Enterprise is credible enough to investigate with Nous, but enterprise readiness should be judged against the exact contract, build, identity integration, data routes and operational demonstration, not the announcement alone.
+
+## 3. Other serious open or self-hostable options
+
+These products serve different jobs. Their core offerings are currently published or commercially offered, unless stated otherwise; a separate formal GA announcement/date was **not found** in the live documentation used. Self-hostable does not necessarily mean OSI open source, and paid governance features are often a separate edition.
+
+**Onyx, formerly Danswer:** the closest fit here to a shared company knowledge workspace, combining search, chat, connectors, agents, research and actions. [42] It advertises permission inheritance and enterprise identity/deployment/support options. [43][44] Its core is MIT, while `ee` directories have a proprietary enterprise licence. [45] Business is **$20/user/month billed annually**; Enterprise is contact sales, not the same published price. [43] It offers a clearer procurement path than a personal-agent runtime, but connector permissions, index freshness and action credentials still require testing. Its July critical MCP-token disclosure is discussed below. [48]
+
+**Open WebUI with tools:** a self-hostable interface around model providers, local inference and tool integrations, with enterprise documentation covering identity, access control, auditing and hardening. It can be a useful controlled staff-facing AI front end, but turning it into a cross-application workspace requires integration work. [51] Current licensing is **not simply BSD for every version**: the vendor identifies a branding-preservation condition in its newer licence and a threshold affecting removal of branding, while normal internal use keeping the branding is not capped at 50 users. The licence change took effect on **19 April 2025**, so that dated policy is older than six months and should be checked against the installed version. [52]
+
+**n8n AI agents:** most attractive when IT wants explicit workflows combining AI decisions with known integrations, rather than an assistant holding broad standing authority. Self-hosted and hosted commercial plans exist; the enterprise feature documentation covers SSO, roles, environments, external secrets and operational features. [53][54] Community is **source-available under the Sustainable Use License**, not conventional OSI open source. Its FAQ, updated **6 October 2026**, permits free internal workflow creation/editing and explains when external editing, hosting as a service or paid features require commercial licensing. It is a serious automation candidate, but workflow design, credentials and recovery remain operator responsibilities. [55]
+
+**LibreChat agents:** an MIT-licensed shared chat/agent application, with custom agents, tools and knowledge features. [56][59] Its documentation supports OIDC and SAML authentication, while its access model describes users, groups, roles and resource permissions. [57][58] That makes it more relevant to a staff-facing front end than a single personal Gateway. However, the access-control page still describes an admin panel as upcoming, so the presence of an authorisation model should not be translated into a claim that every administrative workflow has a complete released UI. Public contractual enterprise support and a support SLA were **not found** in the sources reviewed. [58]
+
+**Dify:** an application/workflow platform for building retrieval and agent applications, with Community and commercial Enterprise routes. Enterprise advertises SSO, RBAC, SCIM, audit/SIEM integration and private deployment. [60] The repository identifies a Dify licence based on Apache 2.0 **with additional conditions**, so it should not be described as unmodified Apache 2.0. [61] It suits a team building defined internal AI applications; it does not remove the need to author, test and operate them. Enterprise price and independently verified current production assurances were **not found** in the sources used.
+
+**Goose:** a developer-focused agent, originating at Block and now described by its project site as an Agentic AI Foundation/Linux Foundation project, under Apache 2.0. It offers desktop/CLI use, model choice, local inference routes, MCP extensions and reusable recipes. It is a strong engineering experimentation candidate for code and defined tool workflows. A packaged company knowledge index, central 300-user workplace administration and a published Goose-specific enterprise support SLA were **not found** on the project page. Its governance and developer adoption should not be mistaken for those product capabilities. [62]
+
+**NemoClaw/OpenShell as an adjunct:** worth evaluating alongside these runtimes because controls are enforced outside the agent harness. NVIDIA's current page describes blueprints for OpenClaw, Hermes and LangChain agents, with filesystem, network, credential and tool restrictions through OpenShell. This is broader than the original OpenClaw-only description. Current formal GA status and a packaged 300-person support contract were **not found** on that page. It can form part of a safer architecture, but does not independently supply company knowledge permissions, employee lifecycle management or an executed service contract. [63]
+
+## 4. Readiness verdict for a 300-person UK company in October 2026
+
+### The decision
+
+**Do not make vanilla OpenClaw or vanilla Hermes the default autonomous workspace for all 300 staff.** Both warrant useful experiments by technically competent teams, but their agent capabilities exceed the evidence for ready-made organisation-wide governance. OpenClaw's own trust model and OCE's explicit pilot status are decisive. Hermes's managed business route helps operations, while the Enterprise proposition still needs procurement evidence. [4][9][32][36]
+
+**For a broad company-knowledge pilot, investigate Onyx Enterprise first among the options in this report.** Its documented identity, permission-aware retrieval and audit routes better match that use case. For a bounded business process, investigate n8n with appropriate commercial controls. For developer operations or a small capable research team, OpenClaw, Hermes and Goose may be more flexible. This is a use-case judgement, not a claim that Onyx universally outperforms the others or reproduces every Amazon Quick capability. [43][44][47][54][62]
+
+Onyx's enterprise proposition comes with a significant qualification: on **20 July 2026**, its project published **GHSA-q62f-rv3h-f822**, a **Critical** cross-user OAuth-token leak in per-user MCP-server endpoints. An ordinary authenticated user could obtain another user's token under the affected configuration. Versions below `4.0.0` are affected; `4.0.0+` is patched. The remediation also requires attention to exposed/stored tokens, not merely a software upgrade. This is directly relevant to the promise of per-user access and prevents a blanket clean-security verdict. [48]
+
+Onyx's current self-hosted audit stream is documented for **v4.3 and later**, and current enterprise permission documentation identifies further group/resource controls for **v4.7 and later**. Therefore, a deployment merely above the July patch floor does not automatically include today's documented controls. Pin and test a current supported version. Audit events cover authentication, access-management/configuration changes and credential access; SIEM retention/forwarding requires an operator-controlled pipeline. Logging is designed not to interrupt requests when it fails, so absence of a record must not automatically be read as absence of an action. [46][47]
+
+### Enterprise-criteria scorecard
+
+Scores are an assessment of **documented fit**, not certification or penetration-test results. **0:** suitable native capability/commitment not found. **1:** local/DIY or weakly specified. **2:** partial, advertised without detailed assurance, or pilot maturity. **3:** concretely documented enterprise route, still subject to configuration and contract. **4:** strong capability independently validated for the target deployment. No product was deployed or independently validated in this study, so none receives 4. Missing evidence does not prove a feature is absent. No summed score is used because data leakage and uncontrolled actions cannot be traded away for a low licence price.
+
+The two enterprise columns prevent capabilities advertised for a different edition being credited to the vanilla agent.
+
+| Enterprise criterion | OpenClaw vanilla | OpenClaw Enterprise, pre-1.0 | Hermes vanilla | Hermes Enterprise, advertised | Onyx Enterprise, current self-hosted |
+| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
+| SSO and employee identity | **1:** trusted-operator access, not a demonstrated employee SSO service. [4] | **2:** documented OIDC, pilot maturity. [9][10] | **0:** enterprise IdP SSO not found in core security docs. [36] | **2:** SSO advertised; detailed protocol/mapping specification not found. [32] | **3:** OIDC/SAML offered. [43] |
+| RBAC and data/action permissions | **1:** tool restrictions within one trust boundary. [4] | **2:** documented principals, roles and grants; pilot. [11] | **1:** allowlists/profiles, not proven organisation-wide RBAC. [36][37] | **1:** fine-grained Enterprise specification not found; Business roles concern team administration. [32] | **3:** groups/resource controls and permission-aware retrieval; version-sensitive. [44][46] |
+| Audit logs and investigation | **1:** operator logging needed; full enterprise trail not established. [4] | **2:** control-plane events; important coverage/export gaps. [12] | **1:** session artefacts need an audit pipeline; compliance-grade stream not found. [30] | **0:** public Enterprise audit/export specification not found. [32] | **3:** documented structured self-hosted audit stream; forwarding and reliability require work. [47] |
+| Data residency and data-flow control | **2:** runtime location controlled, external services still matter. [2][3] | **2:** own infrastructure, but end-to-end locality depends on configuration. [9][13] | **2:** self-hosted execution available; provider/tool egress remains. [30] | **2:** private deployment offered; full residency contract not found. [32][33] | **3:** private/region-specific deployment and local-model routes documented. [43][44] |
+| Support SLA and commercial recourse | **0:** first-party contract not found. | **0:** first-party contract not found. [9] | **0:** core-project contract not found. | **2:** SLAs advertised; measurable terms not found. [32] | **3:** enterprise support/SLA route exists; exact numerical SLA must be obtained. [43][49][50] |
+| Security track record and present assurance | **1:** serious history, recent fixes/audit, residual skill risk. [21][23][24] | **1:** very short independent operational history and documented integration limits. [9][13] | **1:** verified CVE; enterprise assurance not established. [36][38] | **1:** limited public enterprise assurance; private-contract protections unverified. [32][38] | **2:** enterprise controls documented, but July critical cross-user token leak is material. [44][48] |
+| Cost visibility and operating burden | **1:** code free, full cost unknown. [5] | **1:** code free, control-plane operations add work. [9] | **1:** code free, recurring model/tool/operations costs. [29][35] | **1:** enterprise quote and full cost not found. [32] | **2:** a public Business benchmark exists; Enterprise and operating costs need a quote. [43] |
+
+The table's Enterprise scores are deliberately conditional. Hermes Business is available separately, with team billing/administration; those controls are not evidence of everything in the Enterprise column. Onyx's stronger documentary fit does not remove the obligation to demonstrate permissions and revoke leaked credentials.
+
+### Identity, administration and audit: what the proof must look like
+
+For this company, the acceptance exercise should use its actual Entra ID or Google identity setup. Demonstrate new-starter access, group changes, offboarding, session/token revocation and MFA enforcement. Then show that an employee denied access to a source document cannot retrieve it through an agent, copied memory, a shared skill or a cached index. SSO authenticates a person; it does not by itself enforce source permissions or constrain an agent's downstream credentials. This proposed test plan follows from the different identity, permissions and memory models documented above. [4][11][37][44]
+
+Administrative ownership must extend to credentials, connectors, tools, models, schedules, generated skills and spending. Require a way to identify who approved an action and whose authority the action used. Test whether logs reach a separately controlled destination and whether retention, access and failure handling are acceptable. OCE's database events and Onyx's SIEM stream have materially different coverage and operational requirements; neither should be described simply as having audit logs without those details. [12][47]
+
+### UK data protection and residency
+
+Use a data-flow assessment covering source documents, prompts, outputs, memories, embeddings, files, browser/search services, telemetry, backups and remote support. Establish controller/processor responsibilities, purpose, lawful basis, retention/deletion and applicable international-transfer arrangements. Perform a DPIA where the processing is likely to create high risks to individuals, rather than claiming that all AI use automatically requires one. The ICO's AI accountability guidance supports this approach, but its page is under review following the Data (Use and Access) Act; publication/update date was **not found**, so current legal interpretation should be checked during procurement. [64]
+
+UK hosting of the agent alone is insufficient if it sends company material to an overseas model, hosted tool or monitoring service. For Hermes Portal, obtain written confirmation of the applicable Privacy Mode or separate contractual treatment. For private deployments, confirm that external API calls, support access and backups match the intended residency claim. The respective provider lists, hosting FAQ and policy demonstrate why the full route matters. [3][35][39]
+
+### Support, liability and the real cost of free software
+
+The open-agent licence is a software permission, not an operating service. A small IT team becomes responsible for patching, credentials, backups, logs, connectors, evaluation and incident response unless a provider accepts those obligations in a contract. Public community support or a large donor is not a response-time commitment.
+
+Require an enterprise order form to specify support hours, severity definitions, response/restoration targets, patch support, security-notification obligations, subcontractors, data processing, exit/export, liability caps and any indemnities. Onyx's self-hosted agreement is updated **1 July 2025**, older than six months, and cannot establish the current negotiated bargain. Its linked SLA could not be extracted into reliable numerical commitments, so uptime and response-time figures are **not found** here. Hermes's ordinary Portal terms likewise cannot establish a private enterprise agreement. [49][50][40]
+
+Compare **licence/subscription + compute/storage + inference + tool APIs + integration + operational labour + assurance + recovery**. This is a budgeting framework, not a price forecast. As a limited arithmetic benchmark, 300 users at Onyx's displayed Business price would be **$6,000/month on annual billing**. That is **not** an Onyx Enterprise quote, a self-hosted total, or proof that required SSO/support are included in that tier. No credible all-in monthly cost for this company's OpenClaw, Hermes or Onyx deployment was found. [43]
+
+### Who should experiment, and who should defer
+
+**Suitable now:** an IT-led skunkworks team with a named owner, security input and capacity to operate isolated workloads. Proposed initial examples are synthesising public information, working on synthetic business records, drafting an internal briefing from an approved non-sensitive folder, or analysing a disposable copy of a code repository. Give the agent a narrow goal, bounded expenditure and reversible outputs. These are proposed pilot uses, consistent with the NCSC's low-risk approach. [28]
+
+**Defer broad autonomous deployment:** organisations without an owner for patching and incident response, teams unable to explain the agent's credentials and data destinations, or departments proposing unsupervised work on payroll, health data, disciplinary cases, payment instructions, legal commitments or privileged production systems. This is a risk-based recommendation for the stated company, not a claim that these use cases are universally unlawful or technically impossible.
+
+Before expanding, demand working evidence that:
+
+- An unauthorised person cannot reach the agent or obtain another user's data through tools, memory or shared skills.
+- A hostile document or skill cannot cause an unapproved external transfer, credential disclosure or sensitive write.
+- Human approval is tied to a specific intelligible action, not a broad request to trust the agent.
+- An operator can stop work, revoke credentials, investigate activity and restore a known state.
+- Costs, support responsibilities and data-processing terms are understood, including model and tool providers.
+
+These are proposed acceptance outcomes. Passing a prepared demo is insufficient; exercise the failure cases under the intended permissions and deployment. The honest lesson for an AI Transformation Leader is that **open agents can be valuable, but the company is adopting and operating a system of delegated authority, not merely installing a clever chat interface**.
+
